--bc-navy: #0A1020;

This is not necessarily the background of every page.

It is the **brand anchor**.


## 05.7 — THE SITE-SPECIFIC EXTENSION

Colomiers gets a distinctive extension of the Boxing Center visual system:


### Boxing Center Navy

brand identity.


### Ink

near-black editorial tension.


### Bone

human warmth / reading surface.


### Paper

high-legibility information.


### Steel

unresolved routes / secondary information.


### Signal Red

selection / commitment / resolution.

This is how Colomiers becomes a recognizable sibling rather than either:

1. a disconnected new brand; or 2. another carbon copy of existing Boxing Center websites.


## 05.8 — COLOR PHILOSOPHY

Color has **semantic responsibility**.

This is critical.

Red does not mean:

boxing.

Red means:


### A DECISION HAS BECOME ACTIVE.

That one rule gives the accent real purpose.


## 05.9 — CORE PALETTE

Recommended digital palette:

:root { --color-ink: #07090D; --color-navy: #0A1020;

--color-bone: #F4F1EA; --color-paper: #FBFAF7; --color-white: #FFFFFF; --color-steel: #AEBCCF; --color-muted: #A7ADBA; --color-signal: #E21D2F; --color-signal-deep:#B20F20; --color-line-dark: rgba(255,255,255,.16); --color-line-light: rgba(7,9,13,.16); }


### Important

The semantic roles are **locked**.

If the official Boxing Center production red differs from `#E21D2F`, the exact official color should replace the provisional digital red globally.

Do not maintain two visually near-identical reds.


## 05.10 — SIGNAL RED USAGE

Signal red may appear in:

- selected route;
- active state;
- current decision;
- primary resolved CTA;
- selected radio/button;
- important active marker;
- final destination state;
- meaningful validation where appropriate.

It must **not** become:

- every heading;
- every button;
- every divider;

- every icon;
- every hover;
- every card border;
- every background.

Target visual presence across a normal viewport:

**approximately 3–8%.**

Enough to create recognition.

Not enough to saturate.


## 05.11 — ACCESSIBLE RED RULE

Bright signal red is primarily graphical/selection color.

Do not assume bright red text on light backgrounds is sufficiently readable.

For normal red text on Bone/Paper, prefer the deeper signal tone.

White on our proposed signal red has sufficient contrast for ordinary interface text in our current token proposal, while the bright red should not be used indiscriminately as small text against light surfaces.

Formal implementation must verify all real foreground/background combinations.

WCAG 2.2 requires at least **4.5:1 for ordinary text** and **3:1 for large text** at Level AA.

We aim above the threshold where practical rather than designing exactly to the decimal minimum.


## 05.12 — RED ON BLACK WARNING

Avoid relying on red-on-black text combinations.

Even where numbers might approach minimum contrast at larger sizes, this pairing can produce poor real-world perception.

Use:

**white/steel on dark**

and reserve red for graphical state or sufficiently large, tested emphasis.


## 05.13 — COLOR POLARITY IS NARRATIVE

We do not alternate dark/light merely to create visual variety.

Polarity communicates cognitive state.


### UNRESOLVED

Dark.

INK / NAVY

The visitor has choices.


### EXPLANATION

Light.

BONE / PAPER

Information becomes clearer.


### PHYSICAL EVIDENCE

Photography + neutral interface.

Reality takes over.


### RESOLUTION

High-contrast brand surface with Signal Red.

The route is selected.

This gives color progression meaning.


## 05.14 — HOMEPAGE POLARITY MAP

Recommended:

H01 HERO INK / NAVY unresolved H02 TRUTH BONE clarity H03 INTENT PAPER decision H04 DISCIPLINES INK possibility / energy H05 PROXIMITY ENGINE BONE → NAVY on resolution calculation → certainty H06 DESTINATIONS PAPER / photography physical evidence H07 FIRST SESSION BONE human relief H08 PLANNING

NAVY / PAPER data surfaces utility H09 EVIDENCE PAPER trust H10 CONVERGENCE NAVY resolution H11 FOOTER INK closure

This is a narrative sequence.

Not zebra striping.


## 05.15 — NO GLOBAL DARK-MODE TOGGLE

We do not need a user-selectable light/dark theme merely because modern products often have one.

Our dark and light surfaces have semantic roles inside the art direction.

Automatically reversing the system based on `prefers-color-scheme` would destroy that narrative.

Therefore:

**fixed art-directed palette.**

We still respect:

`prefers-reduced-motion`.

Color preferences/forced-colors must remain usable, but not through a cosmetic theme switch.


## 05.16 — TYPOGRAPHIC THESIS

Typography is:


### DIRECTIONAL SIGNAGE MEETS EDITORIAL SPORTS JOURNALISM

Not “fight poster typography.”

Not “luxury fashion typography.”

Not “startup grotesk.”

The type must feel capable of:

- shouting one sentence;
- explaining a complicated MMA concept;
- displaying schedules;
- labeling a club;
- presenting a precise price;
- resolving a route.


## 05.17 — TYPE FAMILY STRATEGY

We should keep the number of font families low.

Target:

**one primary variable grotesk**

plus, only if genuinely justified,

**one extremely restrained editorial countervoice.**

The design must not depend on five downloaded faces.


## 05.18 — PRIMARY TYPE CHARACTERISTICS

Required:

- grotesk;
- excellent French diacritics;
- strong uppercase;
- readable lowercase;
- variable weight preferred;
- useful numeric forms;
- tabular numerals if available;
- enough personality without novelty;
- robust at 14px;
- beautiful above 100px;
- web-performance viable.

Recommended open implementation candidate:


### Instrument Sans Variable

If we later test a licensed Boxing Center brand typeface that materially improves the system, it can replace this candidate.

But its **role characteristics cannot change**.


## 05.19 — SECONDARY TYPE

Not mandatory.

If after visual prototyping the system needs an editorial interruption, introduce a restrained serif only in **rare human/emotional moments**.

Potential candidate:

**Newsreader**

or an equivalent high-quality editorial serif.

Usage ceiling:

**<5% of textual surface.**

Never use serif merely to imitate fashionable agency websites.


## 05.20 — BETTER DEFAULT: ONE FAMILY FIRST

My recommendation:

Prototype the entire system with **one variable grotesk first**.

Only introduce serif if we can name the problem it solves.

Typography itself does not need to perform novelty.

The route system already provides a distinctive mechanism.


## 05.21 — TYPOGRAPHIC ROLES


### Display

Purpose:

hero / section thesis / final convergence.

Characteristics:

- huge;
- compact line-height;
- strong negative space;
- usually sentence-driven;
- rarely more than 2–4 lines.


### Section Heading

Strong but not cinematic.

Usually 2–7 words.


### Lead

Editorial explanation.

18–26px depending viewport.


### Body

Highly readable.

Not tiny to appear “luxury.”


### UI

Selector labels, navigation, filter controls.

Compact and strong.


### Micro

Metadata, timestamps, source labels.

Never less than comfortably readable.


### Numeric

Planning/time/price/data.

Tabular numerals.

Precision.


## 05.22 — FLUID TYPE TOKENS

Conceptual starting tokens:

--type-micro: clamp(.75rem, .71rem + .12vw, .84rem); --type-ui: clamp(.875rem, .83rem + .15vw, 1rem); --type-body: clamp(1rem, .96rem + .22vw, 1.16rem); --type-lead: clamp(1.18rem, 1.03rem + .7vw, 1.65rem); --type-h3: clamp(1.5rem, 1.15rem + 1.4vw, 2.45rem); --type-h2: clamp(2.35rem, 1.45rem + 3.4vw, 5.2rem); --type-display: clamp(3.8rem, 1.4rem + 7.5vw, 9.8rem);

Exact optical tuning happens against real words in French.

Never lock type scales using lorem ipsum.


## 05.23 — HERO SIZE RULE

A huge heading is allowed only when:

- line breaks are art-directed;
- key words remain comprehensible;
- it does not push utility below an absurd fold;
- mobile has its own composition.

Do not make a 180px headline just because the viewport permits it.


## 05.24 — LINE LENGTH

Body copy target:

**55–75 characters per line** for substantial paragraphs.

Lead copy:

shorter.

Metadata:

as needed.

Huge headings:

art-directed.

No 1300px-wide paragraph.


## 05.25 — CASE SYSTEM


### ALL CAPS

Reserved for:

- navigation;

- micro labels;
- route states;
- concise commands;
- club identifiers.

Example:

BOXING CENTER PORTET


### Sentence case

Body and explanatory content.


### Display

Can use uppercase when the statement is short enough.

Do not uppercase entire multi-paragraph information blocks.


## 05.26 — TRACKING

Large uppercase:

slightly tight or neutral depending chosen face.

Micro uppercase:

slightly positive.

Body:

default optical spacing.

No artificially huge letter spacing because “luxury.”


## 05.27 — TYPE WEIGHT

Recommended system:

400 body 450 lead if supported 500 interface 600 emphasis 700 display / decisive state

Avoid 900-weight sport typography across the whole site.

Power comes from scale/composition, not permanent maximum weight.


## 05.28 — ITALICS

If primary face supports meaningful italics:

use them to indicate human speech/contrast sparingly.

Never use faux italic transform.


## 05.29 — NUMBERS

Schedules and prices are information architecture.

Use:

- tabular numerals;
- aligned columns;
- proper non-breaking spaces;
- 29 €, not inconsistent 29€ / 29 EURO / €29;
- real French typography.

Time:

18h40

consistently.

Not mixed with:

`18:40`, `6:40 PM`, etc.


## 05.30 — GRID SYSTEM


### Desktop ≥ 1280px

12 columns.

Suggested:

outer margin: clamp(32px, 4vw, 72px) column gap: clamp(16px, 1.6vw, 28px)


### Medium / tablet

8 columns.


### Mobile

4 columns.

Minimum comfortable side margin:

approximately 20–24px.

Not 12px edge hugging.


## 05.31 — GRID IS NOT A PRISON

The grid aligns information.

It does not require every element to begin/end on a column.

Approved controlled violations:

- hero display type;
- full-bleed photography;
- route graphics;
- selected destination object;
- oversized section numbers.

Violations must feel intentional.


## 05.32 — COMPOSITIONAL ASYMMETRY

Avoid permanent:

50% image 50% text

section after section.

Use asymmetric spans:

7 / 5 8 / 4 5 / 7 3 / 6 / 3

and deliberate negative space.

The page should feel composed.

Not assembled.


## 05.33 — VERTICAL SPACING SYSTEM

Base spacing tokens:

--space-1: .25rem; /* 4 */ --space-2: .5rem; /* 8 */ --space-3: .75rem; /* 12 */ --space-4: 1rem; /* 16 */ --space-5: 1.5rem; /* 24 */ --space-6: 2rem; /* 32 */ --space-7: 3rem; /* 48 */ --space-8: 4.5rem; /* 72 */ --space-9: 6rem; /* 96 */ --space-10: 9rem; /* 144 */ --space-11: 12rem; /* 192 */

Section spacing becomes fluid.

Desktop sections may use 96–192px vertical rhythm.

Mobile:

64–112px.

Not every section same.


## 05.34 — SPACING PRINCIPLE

Spacing expresses relationship.

Closer:

related.

Farther:

new thought.

Do not use fixed 80px gaps everywhere.


## 05.35 — THE ROUTE VISUAL GRAMMAR

This is the signature.

Route graphics have four primitives:


### LINE

relationship.


### NODE

place / state / decision.


### BRANCH

possibility.


### TERMINUS

resolved destination.

Everything else derives from these.


## 05.36 — LINE WEIGHTS

Recommended:

1px latent / background relationship 2px available path 3–4px active/selected path

No 12px spaghetti lines.

The route should feel drawn by an information designer, not a subway-map theme pack.


## 05.37 — ROUTE COLOR STATES


### Latent

Steel at low opacity.


### Available

Steel / Ink depending surface.


### Hover/focus

stronger contrast + local node change.


### Selected

Signal Red.


### Completed

Navy/Ink + red terminus.


### Disabled/unavailable

muted dash pattern + explicit textual status.

Color never works alone.

Line style/node state also changes.


## 05.38 — NODE STATES


### Origin

small solid node.


### Candidate

hollow node.


### Hover/focus

expanded ring.


### Selected

filled signal node.


### Destination

larger structured terminus.


### Unavailable

cross/dash pattern.

Keep it geometric.

No tiny boxing-glove symbols.


## 05.39 — ROUTE CORNERS

Avoid playful rounded subway curves everywhere.

Recommended mix:

- mostly controlled linear geometry;
- occasional smooth bezier transition where motion benefits;
- route turns feel precise.

Think:

**technical drawing**

more than:

**metro map.**


## 05.40 — ROUTE LABELS

Labels sit adjacent to the geometry.

Never rotate long French labels at unreadable angles.

Examples:

COLOMIERS MMA PORTET-SUR-GARONNE CE SOIR

Route geometry supports language.

Language remains primary.


## 05.41 — ROUTE DECORATION RULE

If a route line does not encode:

- sequence;
- choice;
- relationship;
- destination;
- time;
- progress,

it should not exist.

No random line spaghetti behind headings.

This is perhaps the most important anti-drift rule in the design system.


## 05.42 — BORDERS

Borders are structural.

Base:

`1px`.

Selected:

`2px` or stronger color.

Never thick card borders for decoration.


## 05.43 — RADIUS PHILOSOPHY

The site is **not a rounded-card system**.

Default radius:

0–4px

for most interface surfaces.

Photographs:

0–8px depending composition.

Large containers:

maximum roughly 8–12px where the material genuinely calls for softness.


## 05.44 — PILLS

Pills allowed only for actual token-like information:

- filter chip;
- status;
- selection;
- compact metadata.

Not:

every navigation item every CTA every card heading.


## 05.45 — BUTTON LANGUAGE

Buttons should feel like **commands**, not pills.

Primary:

rectangular or near-rectangular.

High contrast.

Strong label.

Directional arrow.

Example:

TROUVER MON CLUB →

not:

✨ Découvrir maintenant ✨


## 05.46 — PRIMARY CTA

Default unresolved context:

dark/light contrast.

Once a choice is resolved:

Signal Red may become primary CTA background.

This visually says:

**this is now the path you've selected.**


## 05.47 — SECONDARY CTA

Usually text + line/arrow.

Do not create a second giant outlined button beside every primary button.

Example:

Primary:

TROUVER MON CLUB →

Secondary:

Voir les disciplines


## 05.48 — BUTTON HEIGHT

Desktop:

roughly **48–56px**.

Mobile:

**52–60px** preferred for primary actions.

WCAG 2.2's minimum pointer target criterion is 24×24 CSS pixels with specified exceptions, but our intended touch targets should comfortably exceed that floor.


## 05.49 — BUTTON HOVER

No gratuitous glow.

Possible system:

1. route arrow moves 4–6px; 2. fill changes; 3. text shifts only negligibly; 4. selected line extends.

Maximum approximately 150–250ms visual response.

No one-second cinematic hover on a utility button.


## 05.50 — BUTTON PRESSED STATE

Physical.

Slight scale or translation:

`0–1px`.

No bouncy cartoon spring.


## 05.51 — LINK LANGUAGE

Inline links:

clear underline or route-line relationship.

Hover can make underline travel/extend.

External:

arrow ↗ where useful.

Internal:

→.

Do not append icons to every sentence link.


## 05.52 — CARDS: DEFAULT ANSWER IS NO

Before creating a card, ask:

Does this information genuinely represent a self-contained object?

If no:

do not create a card.

Approved card-like objects:

- club destination;
- schedule result;
- recommendation;
- offer/pricing unit;
- coach if needed later.

Not approved by default:

- every paragraph;
- every benefit;
- every FAQ;
- every discipline;
- every statistic.


## 05.53 — SELECTOR ROWS

Intent choices are typographic rows separated by rules.

Example:

01 APPRENDRE À BOXER →

──────────────────────────────────────────────── 02 ESSAYER LE MMA → ──────────────────────────────────────────────── 03 ME REMETTRE EN FORME →

This feels more editorial and less SaaS.


## 05.54 — SELECTED ROW

When selected:

- route line activates;
- number becomes signal red or otherwise gains state;
- label gains weight;
- explicit check/status appears.

Not merely background color.


## 05.55 — FORMS

Forms should look **serious and simple**.

No floating labels unless impeccably implemented.

Prefer visible labels above fields.

Example:

PRÉNOM ──────────────────────────── Eddy

Large touch surface.

Minimal border.

Clear focus.


## 05.56 — INPUT SURFACE

On light:

paper/bone background with dark text.

On dark:

avoid low-contrast translucent glass fields.

Use a real surface.


## 05.57 — PLACEHOLDERS

Examples only.

Never replace labels.

Contrast must remain readable if used.


## 05.58 — ERROR LANGUAGE VISUALLY

Error state cannot be red border alone.

Use:

- icon/marker;
- explicit text;
- red/deep signal where appropriate.

Example:

TÉLÉPHONE ──────────────── 06…

! Vérifie le numéro.


## 05.59 — SUCCESS LANGUAGE

No giant green checkmark that looks disconnected from the system.

Success can use:

- resolved route;
- destination node;
- concise confirmation.

Example:

- ────────
- DEMANDE ENVOYÉE

Green is not required unless semantic conventions demand it.


## 05.60 — FOCUS LANGUAGE

Focus is a first-class visual state.

Not browser outline reluctantly preserved.

Design:

- 2–3px high-contrast ring;
- sufficient offset;
- same geometry language as route-node selection.

Focus and hover must be visibly distinct.


## 05.61 — FOCUS ON DARK

Use light/Signal combination with verified contrast.


## 05.62 — FOCUS ON LIGHT

Use Navy/Ink plus outer signal accent where necessary.


## 05.63 — DO NOT REMOVE OUTLINES

Never:

```text
outline: none;
```

without replacing it with an equal or better visible focus treatment.


## 05.64 — PHOTOGRAPHIC DIRECTION

Photography must feel:


### DOCUMENTARY, CLOSE, PHYSICAL, TRUE

Not campaign-stock perfect.

Wanted:

- real sweat;
- real gloves;
- coach interaction;
- authentic movement;
- rings;
- bags;

- hands;
- feet;
- concentration;
- rest between drills;
- children actually learning;
- women actually training;
- facilities accurately represented.


## 05.65 — WHAT PHOTOS SHOULD NOT DO

Avoid:

- fake victory poses;
- everyone staring at camera;
- excessive crossed-arms coach portrait repetition on public pages;
- staged aggression;
- fake punches toward lens;
- stock photography;
- visibly generated people;
- dramatic compositing that makes documentary claims unclear.

The coach-poster campaign system is a separate campaign grammar.

The website should feel more documentary.


## 05.66 — IMAGE COLOR TREATMENT

Base:

natural.

Slight controlled contrast.

Neutral/cool shadows acceptable.

Skin tones preserved.

No aggressive teal-orange grade.

No uniform black-and-white just because boxing photography looks “serious.”


## 05.67 — BLACK-AND-WHITE

Allowed when it has narrative purpose.

Potential uses:

- historical imagery;
- latent/unselected state;
- transition between choice and resolution.

Interesting mechanism:

an unselected club image may begin restrained/desaturated and move toward full natural color upon active selection.

But this should be subtle.


## 05.68 — IMAGE OVERLAYS

Only for:

- text legibility;
- route integration;
- intentional state.

No universal dark 60% overlay.

Better composition is preferred over fixing bad text placement with black gradients.


## 05.69 — IMAGE ASPECT SYSTEM

Approved common ratios:

16:10 / 16:9

wide environmental 3:2 documentary landscape 4:5 portrait / coach / person 1:1 rare, data/objective use

Avoid an accidental zoo of twelve aspect ratios.


## 05.70 — MOBILE CROPS ARE ART-DIRECTED

Do not use desktop center-crop blindly.

Each important asset should support focal data:

{ focalX: 0.62, focalY: 0.38 }

or equivalent CMS/content metadata.

Desktop and mobile may use distinct source crops.


## 05.71 — NEVER CUT THE INFORMATION

Cropping rules:

- do not amputate gloved hands in meaningful technique imagery;
- do not cut coach face;

- do not remove the equipment proving the caption;
- do not crop facility photo until the claimed facility disappears.

Aesthetic crop cannot destroy evidentiary value.


## 05.72 — IMAGE CAPTIONS

Location-specific photos should frequently carry factual captions:

BOXING CENTER MINIMES · TOULOUSE

or

RING · BOXING CENTER PORTET

This is both editorial design and entity clarity.


## 05.73 — ALT SYSTEM

Not filename stuffing.

Example good:

Un groupe s'entraîne sur les sacs au Boxing Center Minimes à Toulouse.

Bad:

club boxe Colomiers boxe anglaise Colomiers boxing center MMA sport combat

The brief requests descriptive ALT attributes; they must genuinely describe the image rather than act as hidden keyword fields.


## 05.74 — DECORATIVE IMAGES

If purely decorative:

empty alt.

Do not force SEO descriptions onto decorative material.


## 05.75 — ICONOGRAPHY

Icon family:

- geometric;
- minimal;
- 1.5–2px stroke;
- square coordinate grid;
- consistent optical size.

Use icons only when they accelerate interpretation.


## 05.76 — FORBIDDEN ICON LANGUAGE

No generic:

🥊 🔥 💪 ⚡

inside primary site UI.

Those are social-caption language.

Not the design-system language.


## 05.77 — DOMAIN ICONS

If needed, abstract:

- ring;
- cage;
- child;
- clock;
- location;
- level.

But text usually remains stronger.

Do not turn disciplines into tiny pictograms if the pictograms require decoding.


## 05.78 — ARROWS ARE CORE ICONS

Arrow system:

→ internal progression.

↗ external physical club/site.

↓ continuation only where useful.

← back.

These become part of the route grammar.


## 05.79 — DATA VISUALIZATION LANGUAGE

We will have:

- route recommendation;
- schedules;
- availability;
- comparisons;
- possibly discipline frequency.

The visualization style remains consistent.


## 05.80 — DATA RULE #1

Text answer first.

Graphic enhancement second.

Example:

Portet corresponds to your MMA + evening criteria.

Then visualization.

Not a cryptic chart requiring interpretation.


## 05.81 — DATA RULE #2

No decorative charts.

If an infographic does not help decide:

remove it.


## 05.82 — SCHEDULE LANGUAGE

Time is represented through:

- aligned numerals;
- clear day labels;
- route-line/timeline primitive;
- club label;
- discipline;
- level where useful.

Not calendar-box sludge.


## 05.83 — COMPARISON LANGUAGE

Minimes / Portet:

Use rows representing criteria.

Example:

MINIMES PORTET MMA — YES BOXE ANGLAISE YES YES ...

But only verified facts.

Visual form may be more editorial than literal table on desktop.

Semantic HTML can still be a table where the information is tabular.


## 05.84 — DO NOT HIDE ABSENCE

If a club does not offer something:

show it clearly.

A dash can be visually elegant.

Truth is part of the visual system.


## 05.85 — NEGATIVE SPACE

Negative space is active.

It creates:

- pauses;
- hierarchy;
- confidence.

But “luxury emptiness” cannot make the user scroll 800px through nothing.

Every void must support composition.


## 05.86 — MATERIAL SYSTEM

We use four visual materials.


### INK

Deep dark surface.

Tension.


### PAPER

Clean factual reading surface.


### PHOTOGRAPH

Reality.


### LINE

Relationship.

That is enough.

No chrome.

No glass.

No fake leather.

No canvas texture simply because boxing equipment uses canvas.


## 05.87 — MICROTEXTURE

Potential extremely subtle paper/noise texture may exist on large flat fields.

Requirements:

- barely perceptible;
- CSS/procedural/tiny compressed asset;
- does not harm text;
- not “film grain overlay” across everything.

Target:

felt, not seen.


## 05.88 — SHADOWS

Minimal.

We do not build depth through giant soft shadows.

If elevation needed:

small controlled shadow or contrast boundary.

Physicality comes from imagery/composition, not Material Design elevation stacks.


## 05.89 — DIVIDERS

Route line can often replace generic horizontal rules.

Where plain divider is better:

use plain divider.

Do not force the branded mechanism everywhere.


## 05.90 — CURSOR


### NO GLOBAL CUSTOM CURSOR.

This is important.

Custom cursors frequently damage:

- predictability;
- accessibility;
- input switching;
- performance;
- browser conventions.

Default cursor stays.


## 05.91 — POINTER-FINE ENRICHMENT

On desktop pointer devices, specific spatial visualizations may add an auxiliary cursor label.

Example:

hover destination:

PORTET VOIR →

But:

- native pointer remains understandable;

- touch receives equivalent information;
- reduced-motion mode does not depend on it;
- no full-site circle chasing the pointer.


## 05.92 — HOVER PHILOSOPHY

Hover reveals **consequence**, not decoration.

Good:

hover `PORTET` → relevant route highlights.

Good:

hover schedule → club/time relationship clarifies.

Bad:

everything tilts in 3D.

Bad:

text randomly scrambles.

Bad:

images zoom 1.2× everywhere.


## 05.93 — TEXT SCRAMBLE

Forbidden as default animation.

It damages comprehension.

Could potentially exist in a one-off decorative label if it communicates uncertainty/resolution.

But default:

no.


## 05.94 — MAGNETIC BUTTONS

Default:

no.

They have become creative-site cliché and can make pointer behavior feel imprecise.

Our brand concept is **precision**.

Buttons should stay where the user expects them.


## 05.95 — IMAGE PARALLAX

Allowed only very subtly.

Maximum perceived movement should remain controlled.

No background swimming independently of page at exaggerated speed.


## 05.96 — SECTION TRANSITIONS

Transitions happen through:

- polarity;
- route continuation;
- typography;
- cropping;
- spatial alignment.

Not through generic diagonal SVG waves.


## 05.97 — DARK → LIGHT TRANSITION

The route can physically cross the polarity boundary.

Example:

dark section:

white/steel path.

At boundary:

path becomes Ink.

Selected Signal state remains consistent.

This small detail can make the site feel impossibly coherent.


## 05.98 — THE ROUTE MUST SURVIVE ALL SURFACES

Dark:

light route.

Light:

dark route.

Photo:

local contrast backing or offset label.

Never render an important route line invisibly over photography.


## 05.99 — MOBILE VISUAL SYSTEM

Mobile is not a visual reduction.

It uses the same primitives:

- line;
- node;
- branch;
- terminus;
- typography;
- photography;
- polarity.

But relationships become more sequential.


## 05.100 — MOBILE GRID

4 columns.

Most primary text:

4 columns.

Supporting labels:

2–3 columns where appropriate.

Full-bleed image:

controlled edge escape.


## 05.101 — MOBILE DISPLAY TYPE

Do not shrink huge desktop typography to 28px.

Still bold.

Typical display:

roughly 52–72px depending phrase/device.

But utility must appear early.


## 05.102 — MOBILE ROUTES

Routes become more vertical.

This is natural because the user's thumb/scroll direction is vertical.

Do not squeeze complex branching horizontally.

Example:

COLOMIERS

- │ ├── BOXE │ ├── MMA │ └── ENFANT

After selection:

COLOMIERS

- │ MMA │
- PORTET


## 05.103 — MOBILE SELECTORS

One primary choice per visual band.

Large rows.

Minimum target comfortably above WCAG's minimum.

No tiny chips packed edge-to-edge.


## 05.104 — MOBILE PHOTOGRAPHY

Use portrait crops intentionally.

No 21:9 letterboxes on phones.

Photography should feel close.


## 05.105 — RESPONSIVE ASYMMETRY

Desktop asymmetry may transform into sequence.

Example desktop:

PHOTO TEXT ROUTE

Mobile:

TEXT ROUTE PHOTO

Responsive behavior follows comprehension.

Not CSS stacking order by accident.


## 05.106 — VISUAL ACCESSIBILITY IS NOT A SECOND THEME

The accessible version is the design.

There is no:

normal version

and

accessible fallback.

Focus, contrast, touch areas and reduced motion belong inside the art direction.


## 05.107 — CONTRAST STANDARD

Minimum WCAG AA:

- 4.5:1 normal text;
- 3:1 large text;
- UI/non-text contrast requirements handled as applicable.

W3C also notes that thin/unusual fonts can render perceptually weaker even where nominal mathematical contrast passes, so our typography should not be excessively thin.

Therefore:

we do not use 200-weight pale-grey 14px text.

Period.


## 05.108 — TEXT RESIZE

Layouts must survive text enlargement.

WCAG requires text to be resizable up to 200% without loss of content or functionality under the applicable criterion.

Thus:

no brittle fixed-height text boxes.


## 05.109 — COLOR CANNOT OWN MEANING

Selected state:

color + weight + marker.

Error:

color + text + marker.

Unavailable:

color + dash/label.

Never:

red means A, blue means B, with no other distinction.


## 05.110 — HIGH-CONTRAST / FORCED-COLOR RESILIENCE

The route system must remain understandable if custom colors disappear.

That means:

- borders;
- text labels;
- selected markers;
- semantic controls

remain meaningful.

Do not draw the entire interaction only as a CSS background image.


## 05.111 — PRINT / READER RESILIENCE

If someone prints a page or extracts it into reader-like text:

the core hierarchy still makes sense.

That is a surprisingly useful quality gate for semantic discipline.


## 05.112 — DESIGN TOKENS: COLOR

Implementation should expose semantic—not component-specific—tokens.

Good:

--surface-primary --surface-inverse --text-primary --text-secondary --route-latent --route-active --border-subtle --focus-ring

Bad:

--mma-card-red --hero-button-blue --footer-line-grey

Semantic tokens make system-wide corrections possible.


## 05.113 — DESIGN TOKENS: TYPE

--font-primary --font-editorial --text-micro --text-ui --text-body

--text-lead --text-h3 --text-h2 --text-display


## 05.114 — DESIGN TOKENS: SPACE

The shared scale from §05.33.

Do not invent `margin-top: 37px` locally unless optical necessity is documented.


## 05.115 — DESIGN TOKENS: BORDER

--border-thin: 1px; --border-active: 2px;


## 05.116 — DESIGN TOKENS: ROUTE

--route-thin: 1px; --route-default: 2px; --route-active: 4px; --node-sm: 8px; --node-md: 12px; --node-active: 16px;

Exact values can scale responsively within controlled limits.


## 05.117 — DESIGN TOKENS: RADIUS

--radius-xs: 2px;

--radius-sm: 4px; --radius-md: 8px;

No `--radius-3xl: 32px` unless a very specific later component justifies it.


## 05.118 — DESIGN TOKENS: Z-INDEX

Predefined layers.

Example:

0 base 10 content 20 sticky local 30 header 40 overlay shade 50 dialog/menu 60 accessibility/system alert

No `z-index: 999999`.


## 05.119 — LOGO SAFE SPACE

Logo receives guaranteed visual isolation.

Header cannot jam links against it.

Photos cannot intersect its legibility zone.

No route line crosses through the official mark.

The route belongs **around** the brand.

Never on top of it.


## 05.120 — COLOMIERS LABEL

Do not create a fake new gym logo reading:

BOXING CENTER COLOMIERS

as though it were a sixth location identity.

Preferred treatment:

[OFFICIAL BOXING CENTER LOGO] DEPUIS COLOMIERS

or another clearly contextual label.

This maintains the entity distinction established in Parts 01–03.


## 05.121 — CLUB DESTINATION LABELS

Real locations can use their actual names:

**BOXING CENTER MINIMES**

**BOXING CENTER PORTET**

These are physical destinations.

The visual hierarchy should make this difference apparent.


## 05.122 — SECTION NUMBERING

Potential subtle editorial device:

01 / ORIGIN 02 / CHOOSE 03 / ROUTE

But we should not over-number every section.

Use only if prototypes prove it contributes to progression.

Do not turn site into PowerPoint.


## 05.123 — MICROCOPY STYLE VISUALLY

Microcopy should appear confident, not apologetic.

Example:

Good:

**AUCUNE SALLE PHYSIQUE À COLOMIERS.**

Not:

Veuillez noter qu'à l'heure actuelle, malheureusement…

Typography reinforces directness.


## 05.124 — LONG COPY

Longer SEO/supporting copy is handled through:

- readable column width;
- meaningful H2/H3;
- pull facts;
- visual breathing room;
- inline route relationships;
- occasional evidence blocks.

Not accordions hiding everything.


## 05.125 — ACCORDIONS

Use only for actual secondary detail.

FAQ can use disclosure patterns where useful.

But core search answers remain visible HTML.

No “expand to read all SEO content.”


## 05.126 — TABLES

Use real HTML tables where the relationship is genuinely tabular.

Style them editorially.

Do not rebuild tables as hundreds of divs merely to animate them.


## 05.127 — PRICING SURFACES

Prices are not ecommerce cards by default.

Treat them as editorial financial information.

Large number.

Clear period.

Clear destination/conditions.

No giant “MOST POPULAR” badge unless genuinely meaningful.


## 05.128 — PRICE TYPOGRAPHY

Example:

259 € 12 MOIS

Not:

€259.00* BEST DEAL!!!

French context and exact terms first.


## 05.129 — TRUST LABELS

Source/freshness metadata receives a consistent visual treatment:

VÉRIFIÉ · 07.09.2026

small but legible.

Potential tiny node preceding it.

This could become a distinctive trust motif.


## 05.130 — SOURCE LANGUAGE

Where factual comparison matters:

SOURCE · BOXING CENTER PORTET

The site should feel comfortable showing where knowledge comes from.

This is part of our GEO architecture too.


## 05.131 — THE “CLAIM CHIP”

Potential reusable microcomponent:

- VÉRIFIÉ

But use sparingly.

Do not badge every sentence.

Priority:

volatile facts.


## 05.132 — HEADER VISUAL

Desktop:

approximately 64–76px initial height depending composition.

Logo left.

Primary nav right/center.

Commencer command.

No giant floating rounded navbar.

The header should feel integrated into architecture.


## 05.133 — STICKY HEADER

On scroll:

reduce vertical density.

Background becomes clear surface with subtle border.

No giant blur glass.

Maybe tiny backdrop support if needed, but solid controlled material preferred.


## 05.134 — MOBILE HEADER

Approximately 56–64px.

Logo/context.

Menu.

No three simultaneous CTAs.


## 05.135 — MOBILE MENU VISUAL

Full-screen designed state.

Not generic slide-in drawer from component library.

It should use:

- Ink/Navy;
- large typographic destinations;
- route spine;
- real club links separated below.

The menu itself becomes a mini knowledge map.


## 05.136 — FOOTER VISUAL

Dark.

Dense but breathable.

Typography carries hierarchy.

Route terminus may create one final visual relationship:

COLOMIERS

- ──────────── NETWORK

Then factual statement.

No newsletter block unless there is an actual newsletter strategy.


## 05.137 — SOCIAL/OG VISUAL EXTENSION

The design system must survive outside the website.

Open Graph image system should use:

- brand logo;
- contextual DEPUIS COLOMIERS;
- large page intent;
- route graphic;
- controlled photography when appropriate.

Examples:

MMA DEPUIS COLOMIERS ──────────

- PORTET

or

BOXE ENFANTS DEPUIS COLOMIERS

No generic screenshot.


## 05.138 — FAVICON

Use official Boxing Center brand mark only if it remains legible at favicon size and the asset is authorized.

Do not invent a “C” location mark that looks like a physical Colomiers club.


## 05.139 — 404 VISUAL

Route terminates unexpectedly.

But instead of huge red error aesthetic:

one route line stops.

Another route quietly offers valid exits.

Copy carries character.

Same system.


## 05.140 — EMPTY STATE VISUAL

A route reaches no matching node.

Use dashed branch.

Text:

**PAS DE CORRESPONDANCE EXACTE.**

Alternatives appear as valid branches.

This is much more coherent than generic empty-state illustration.


## 05.141 — LOADING VISUAL

If necessary:

line progressively evaluates.

Not:

spinner.

But static HTML should minimize how often loading exists.

No fake loader.


## 05.142 — SKELETON VISUAL

Only where real delayed data exists.

Skeleton follows final geometry and does not shimmer aggressively.


## 05.143 — MAPS

If interactive map appears later:

its visual style should not overwhelm the route concept.

Map = geographic utility.

Route = decision grammar.

Do not conflate them.

A real map should look like a map.


## 05.144 — NO FAKE CARTOGRAPHY

Our route visualization must never imply geographic distances accurately unless it truly encodes them.

It is a conceptual decision graph.

Make that distinction visually clear.


## 05.145 — ANTI-AI-SLOP RULE: COMPOSITION

Reject:

perfect symmetric card grids random floating spheres over-smoothed portraits generic volumetric lighting unnecessary gradients impossible shadows random microtext fake technical labels nonsense numbers pseudo-Japanese typography decorative coordinates random barcodes fake tickets generic mesh gradients.

Every unusual artifact needs meaning.


## 05.146 — ANTI-AI-SLOP RULE: TYPOGRAPHY

AI-generated design frequently produces:

- too many font weights;
- oversized words without hierarchy;
- meaningless microcopy;
- badly tracked uppercase;
- random italic serif inserts;
- nonsensical labels.

Our rule:

Every visible word has editorial purpose.


## 05.147 — ANTI-AI-SLOP RULE: SPACING

No “fill the canvas.”

Dead space is allowed when compositional.

Dense space is allowed when informative.

Everything should appear deliberately placed.


## 05.148 — ANTI-AI-SLOP RULE: REPETITION

If three consecutive sections use:

eyebrow heading paragraph 3 cards

redesign.

Repeated component anatomy should indicate repeated information semantics—not designer laziness.


## 05.149 — ANTI-AI-SLOP RULE: VISUAL INVENTION

Never generate or fabricate:

- club spaces;
- equipment;
- staff;
- crowds;
- physical locations;
- trophies;
- historical photographs

and present them as actual Boxing Center evidence.

Stylized abstract graphic material may be generated/designed later.

Documentary proof may not.


## 05.150 — ART DIRECTOR'S “ONE THING” TEST

Screenshot any viewport.

Ask:

What is the one dominant thing?

There must be an answer.

If everything has equal weight:

fail.


## 05.151 — HIERARCHY TEST

Blur the screen heavily.

You should still perceive:

1. main message;

2. dominant action/object; 3. secondary information.

If not:

hierarchy is weak.


## 05.152 — GRAYSCALE TEST

Remove color.

The interface must still communicate:

- hierarchy;
- selected state through weight/shape;
- focus;
- structure.

If everything depends on red:

fail.


## 05.153 — 200% ZOOM TEST

At 200%:

- information survives;
- routes do not obscure text;
- navigation remains operable;
- forms remain usable;
- no essential text clips.


## 05.154 — 320PX TEST

At narrow mobile width:

- no sideways page scroll;
- selector remains understandable;
- no letter-breaking French headline;
- buttons retain labels;
- route graphic transforms appropriately.


## 05.155 — 4K TEST

At huge desktop:

do not simply stretch everything infinitely.

Maximum content widths and art-directed composition preserve density.

Hero can use extra visual field.

Body does not become a 180-character line.


## 05.156 — LOW-END PHONE TEST

Disable expensive visual layer.

The site should still look deliberately designed.

If removing Canvas/WebGL makes the site look unfinished:

the foundation is wrong.


## 05.157 — SCREENSHOT TEST

Static screenshot should still look excellent.

Motion must add.

Not rescue.


## 05.158 — PRINT TEST

The printable informational core should remain understandable.

Useful especially for planning/pricing/contact.

No need to print decorative route motion.


## 05.159 — REFERENCE-FUSION LEDGER — VISUAL SYSTEM

**Layer Source mechanism**

**Operation Boxing Center**

**Colomiers**

**result**

**+1**

Immersive focus

Lusion's concentrated interactive storytelling

subtraction One signature route system rather than effects everywhere

visual object represents unresolved user intent

Type as environment

Locomotive's integrated digital-first art direction

recontextualization typography

behaves as directional signage

```text
type and data
states react to
decision context
```

Effortless complexity

Igloo compression sophisticated recommendation hidden behind simple choices

recommendation explains its logic on demand

Motion infrastructure

Darkroom unified/performance tooling philosophy

technical graft one controlled motion/render system

same primitive changes meaning across space, time and progress

Truth-as-desi gn

Internal Ramonville escalation absence of Colomiers location becomes explicit visual premise

limitation becomes useful orientation product

Documentary conversion

Internal Portet/Minimes

compression real images + first-session storytelling

physical evidence changes based on chosen destination

Strict local claims

Internal Blagnac graft typography distinguishes location context from physical entity

even visual branding prevents accidental sixth-club inference

No single reference can be named from the visible final result.

That remains mandatory.


## 05.160 — THE VISUAL +1

Part 04 named:


### THE RESOLVING ROUTE

Part 05 gives it a visual law:


### RED IS EARNED.

At the start:

no red route has been earned.

The visitor has not decided.

Routes are latent.

Steel.

Unresolved.

As the visitor supplies intent, the visual field narrows.

When a decision becomes genuinely supported by data:

**the route becomes red.**

This is subtle but powerful.

The brand accent therefore becomes the visual representation of **certainty**.

Not decoration.


## 05.161 — WHY “RED IS EARNED” MATTERS

Imagine the homepage opening.

Mostly:

Ink Navy Bone Steel.

No screaming red hero.

Then the visitor chooses:

**MMA.**

One small red state appears.

They choose:

**Evening.**

The path extends.

Recommendation resolves:

**Portet.**

Now the complete path becomes Signal Red.

By the final CTA:

red is visually dominant enough to say:

**GO.**

The website's color hierarchy literally follows the cognitive journey.

This is much more memorable than:

Boxing = red, therefore paint everything red.


## 05.162 — VISUAL SYSTEM IN ONE SENTENCE

**A disciplined editorial interface begins in restrained Boxing Center navy, ink,** **bone and steel; real photography supplies physical intensity; a precise route** **grammar connects decisions; and the brand's red appears only when the** **visitor has earned certainty.**

That is the design system.


## 05.163 — VISUAL IMPLEMENTATION CONTRACT

An implementation LLM may improvise:

- optical spacing within token boundaries;
- responsive crop choice when asset focal metadata permits;
- line wrapping;
- small alignment refinements;

- technically equivalent CSS methods.

It may **not** reinterpret:

- color semantics;
- red-as-resolution rule;
- route primitive meanings;
- logo treatment;
- radius philosophy;
- typography hierarchy;
- no-card default;
- documentary image rule;
- dark/light narrative polarity;
- focus language;
- responsive parity;
- no-fake-Colomiers visual language.

Those are locked.


## 05.164 — PART 05 ACCEPTANCE GATE

Before a visual surface passes:


### Brand

Does it still unmistakably belong to Boxing Center?


### Context

Could it accidentally imply a physical club in Colomiers?

If yes → reject.


### Hierarchy

Can I name the dominant object/message?


### Route

Does any route graphic actually encode something?

If no → delete it.


### Red

Has the interface earned the red?

If no → remove it.


### Photography

Is the image real, useful and correctly contextualized?


### Type

Can it be read quickly on mobile?


### Composition

Does it feel art-directed rather than templated?


### Accessibility

Does the state remain understandable without color?


### Contrast

Does it meet/exceed the required contrast?


### Mobile

Does the mobile composition feel intentionally redesigned?


### Performance

Would the visual system remain complete without expensive JS?


### AI-slop

Could an AI website generator plausibly produce this exact composition from “premium boxing website”?

If yes → push further.


## 05.165 — LOCKED PART 05

We now have the permanent visual grammar:

BRAND Official Boxing Center identity MATERIALS Ink / Paper / Photograph / Line PALETTE Navy / Ink / Bone / Paper / Steel / Signal Red TYPE Precise editorial grotesk GRID 12 → 8 → 4 GEOMETRY Linear / low radius / no SaaS card field ROUTE Line / Node / Branch / Terminus PHOTOGRAPHY Real / documentary / contextualized STATE Latent → Available → Selected → Resolved COLOR LAW RED IS EARNED RESPONSIVE Transform, never amputate

ACCESSIBILITY Part of the visible art direction ANTI-SLOP Nothing decorative without purpose

The combination of:


### PRECISION UNDER PRESSURE


### THE RESOLVING ROUTE


### RED IS EARNED

is now the visual foundation I recommend locking.


<a id="part-06-motion-interaction-system"></a>

# PART 06 — Motion & Interaction System


## 06.0 — PURPOSE

Part 05 established what the interface looks like.

Part 06 defines:

**HOW IT BEHAVES WHEN TIME ENTERS THE DESIGN.**

Motion is not a layer applied after UI.

Motion is responsible for communicating:

- relationship;
- hierarchy;
- causality;

- selection;
- continuity;
- progress;
- arrival;
- feedback.

For Boxing Center Colomiers specifically, motion has one additional responsibility:

**make uncertainty visibly resolve into certainty.**

That is the behavioral expression of:


### THE RESOLVING ROUTE

and:


### RED IS EARNED.


## 06.1 — MOTION THESIS

The motion direction is:


### CONTROLLED IMPACT

Boxing is explosive.

Our interface should not be.

The site's motion should behave more like a technically excellent boxer:

- economical;
- deliberate;
- quick when necessary;
- calm when nothing is happening;
- explosive only at the meaningful moment;
- immediately able to stop/change direction.

No decorative restlessness.


## 06.2 — THE MOST IMPORTANT RULE


### NOTHING MOVES WITHOUT AN INFORMATIONAL REASON.

Every animation must satisfy at least one of these:

ORIENT Where did this come from? CONNECT What is related to what? CONFIRM Did my action work? PRIORITIZE What should I notice? TRANSITION What changed? RESOLVE Which choice survived? CONTINUE Where do I go next?

If the answer is:

“It looks premium.”

reject the animation.


## 06.3 — MOTION IS NOT THE CONTENT

Static state must always be complete.

The animation may explain:

how A became B.

But A and B must both remain understandable without seeing the transition.

This applies especially to:

- recommendation;
- route selection;
- pricing;
- planning;
- availability;
- club comparison;
- errors;
- success;
- page navigation.


## 06.4 — TECHNICAL ARCHITECTURE: MPA FIRST

The default architecture is:


### Astro multi-page application + browser-native navigation.

Pages remain real documents.

Normal links remain normal links.

Normal Back/Forward remains native.

Search engines receive ordinary documents.

No client router is required for basic operation.


## 06.5 — PAGE TRANSITIONS: NATIVE FIRST

Modern browsers now support View Transitions for both same-document and cross-document experiences, allowing visual continuity without manually keeping two application states in the DOM. MDN specifically notes that the API can reduce cognitive discontinuity while avoiding many of the focus/DOM problems historically associated with custom SPA transitions.

Therefore:


### Default

Use **native cross-document View Transitions** where supported.


### Fallback

Normal browser navigation.


### Never

Require transitions for usability.


## 06.6 — WHY NOT <ClientRouter /> BY DEFAULT?

Astro currently distinguishes:

1. native cross-document View Transitions; 2. its `<ClientRouter />` client-side routing layer.

Astro notes that native cross-document transitions preserve the MPA model and do not add client-side routing JavaScript, while `<ClientRouter />` adds benefits such as persistent elements/state but also requires additional lifecycle/script handling. Astro further notes that native browser capabilities are progressively reducing the need for the router.

Therefore:


### DO NOT INSTALL CLIENT-SIDE ROUTING AS A VISUAL EFFECT.

`<ClientRouter />` becomes permissible only if we identify a concrete product requirement that native MPA transitions cannot satisfy cleanly.

Examples:

- persistent recommendation state requiring seamless cross-page DOM persistence;
- a genuinely valuable shared interactive object;
- a technically validated cross-route continuity mechanism impossible to accomplish

elegantly using URLs/session state/native transitions.

Even then:

justify the cost.


## 06.7 — SELECTOR STATE DOES NOT REQUIRE AN SPA

User selection may persist through:

URL parameters + sessionStorage + server-rendered defaults where appropriate

Example:

```text
/quel-club/?objectif=mma&niveau=debutant
```

The URL remains shareable.

The content can remain server understandable.

The site doesn't need an application router merely to remember:

MMA.


## 06.8 — MOTION TECHNOLOGY HIERARCHY

Use the simplest technology capable of producing the intended mechanism.

Priority:

1. CSS TRANSITIONS ↓ 2. CSS KEYFRAMES ↓ 3. WEB ANIMATIONS API ↓ 4. VIEW TRANSITION API ↓ 5. SVG ANIMATION ↓ 6. CANVAS 2D ↓ 7. WEBGL

Moving down this list requires justification.


## 06.9 — CSS IS DEFAULT

Use CSS for:

- hover;
- focus presentation;
- control state;
- header transitions;
- simple reveals;
- opacity;
- translate;

- scale;
- color;
- border;
- route-state color changes.

No JS animation library required.


## 06.10 — WAAPI IS FOR CONTROL

The Web Animations API is appropriate where the animation must be:

- cancelable;
- reversible;
- state-driven;
- sequenced;
- interrupted by input;
- synchronized with DOM updates.

Example:

Proximity Engine convergence.

If the user changes their mind halfway through:

the animation must stop/reverse immediately.

WAAPI is better suited than stacking CSS classes and timeouts.


## 06.11 — VIEW TRANSITIONS ARE FOR CONTINUITY

Use View Transitions for:

- page navigation;
- schedule-filter rearrangement;
- selector-result replacement;
- club comparison changes;
- shared route-object continuity.

Not:

every hover.


## 06.12 — SVG IS THE DEFAULT ROUTE MEDIUM

The Resolving Route should initially be implemented as:


### SVG

Advantages:

- crisp;
- resolution-independent;
- semantic structure possible;
- CSS-controllable;
- tiny payload;
- easy node/path state;
- good responsive control;
- no canvas dependency;
- easy reduced-motion version.

Only abandon SVG if prototyping proves it cannot deliver the desired visual result.


## 06.13 — CANVAS / WEBGL GATE

Canvas/WebGL requires all five answers to be **yes**:

1. Does SVG genuinely fail visually? 2. Does the effect communicate product meaning? 3. Is mobile equally intentional? 4. Can reduced motion replace it completely? 5. Does the performance budget survive?

If any answer is no:

do not ship WebGL.


## 06.14 — NO MOTION LIBRARY BY DEFAULT

Do not install:

- GSAP;
- Motion/Framer Motion;
- Lenis;
- Locomotive Scroll;
- anime.js;
- large choreography frameworks

simply because the site contains motion.

The project does not begin with a motion-library dependency.

Native browser capabilities are sufficient for most of the system.

A later specialized requirement may justify a library.

It must be documented.


## 06.15 — NO REACT JUST FOR ANIMATION

Absolutely prohibited:

Install React + Motion so the SVG lines can animate.

Astro remains framework-light.

Vanilla TypeScript + platform APIs first.

Framework island only if its **product logic**, not its animation, justifies the framework.


## 06.16 — SCROLL PHILOSOPHY


### NATIVE SCROLL IS SACRED.

No scroll-jacking.

No replacement physics.

No mandatory smooth-scroll engine.

No delayed wheel.

No artificial momentum.

No capturing trackpad gestures to move one section at a time.

The page scrolls like a website.


## 06.17 — scroll-behavior: smooth

Even this is not globally necessary.

Anchor navigation can use smooth behavior only where appropriate and must respect reduced motion.

Default browser behavior is acceptable.


## 06.18 — SCROLL-DRIVEN MOTION

Scroll can influence:

- route progress;

- section reveal;
- image crop;
- subtle relationship changes.

It must not:

- control basic text readability;
- lock user into a sequence;
- create essential hidden states;
- move backgrounds aggressively;
- create vestibular-heavy parallax.

W3C specifically identifies unnecessary scroll-triggered movement and parallax as potential motion-sensitivity problems.


## 06.19 — SCROLL DISTANCE ↔ MOTION DISTANCE

Rule:

**The farther the page scrolls, the less an individual object should travel.**

Long scroll + large independent parallax = unpleasant.

Preferred scroll transformations:

translate: 8–32px scale: 0.98–1 opacity: subtle route: progressive local reveal

Not:

translate: 600px rotate: 120deg scale: 0.4 → 2


## 06.20 — PERFORMANCE PROPERTIES

As a default, high-frequency animation should primarily use:

- transform;

- opacity.

web.dev recommends these because they are typically handled at the compositing stage, whereas animation of geometry/layout or expensive paint properties can trigger substantially more rendering work. It also cautions against indiscriminate `will-change`, which can itself waste memory/resources.


## 06.21 — EXCEPTIONS

Not every animation can be transform/opacity.

For example:

SVG route drawing via stroke dash may involve paint.

That is acceptable when:

- the animated area is limited;
- the event is short;
- there are few paths;
- profiling confirms it remains smooth.

We optimize intentionally.

We do not turn a performance guideline into superstition.


## 06.22 — MOTION TOKEN SYSTEM

No component invents its own timing.

The entire site uses shared timing families.

:root {

--motion-instant: 80ms; --motion-fast: 140ms; --motion-control: 200ms; --motion-medium: 300ms; --motion-large: 420ms; --motion-section: 600ms; --motion-hero: 900ms; }


## 06.23 — WHAT EACH TOKEN MEANS


### 80ms — instant

Pressed-state microfeedback.


### 140ms — fast

Hover/focus visual response.


### 200ms — control

Button states, node selection, filter state.


### 300ms — medium

Menu/UI-panel changes.


### 420ms — large

Recommendation convergence / significant component transformation.


### 600ms — section

Large controlled entrance.

Rare.


### 900ms — hero

Maximum orchestration window.

Not a delay.

Content remains usable before it finishes.


## 06.24 — NO ARBITRARY DURATIONS

Prohibited:

237ms 473ms 1100ms

unless an optical/prototyping reason is documented.

Motion should feel like one system.


## 06.25 — EASING TOKENS

Recommended starting system:

:root { --ease-standard: cubic-bezier(.2, .8, .2, 1); --ease-enter:

cubic-bezier(.16, 1, .3, 1); --ease-exit: cubic-bezier(.4, 0, 1, 1); --ease-emphasized: cubic-bezier(.65, 0, .35, 1); }


## 06.26 — EASING MEANINGS


### Standard

general controlled response.


### Enter

fast arrival → gentle settling.


### Exit

decisive departure.


### Emphasized

meaningful transformation.

Used very sparingly.


## 06.27 — NO “BOUNCE” LANGUAGE

Bouncy springs generally conflict with:


### PRECISION UNDER PRESSURE.

We don't want the interface behaving like rubber.

If a physical spring is used:

- high damping;
- essentially no oscillation;
- no overshoot beyond a few pixels.


## 06.28 — SPRING POLICY

A spring is allowed when it models:

- drag settling;
- physically continuous object movement;
- cancelable positional response.

Not:

headlines.

Not:

every button.

Not:

page transitions.

Not:

route drawing.


## 06.29 — DELAY POLICY

User-generated actions:


### NO PERCEPTIBLE ARTIFICIAL DELAY.

Input feedback starts immediately.

Maximum deliberate delay after direct input:

approximately **0–50ms**.

Never:

click → wait 500ms → animation starts.


## 06.30 — STAGGER POLICY

Stagger is dangerous because it creates “agency website syndrome.”

Allowed:

3–5 closely related items.

Interval:

approximately:

30–60ms

Maximum total stagger:

~200ms.

Do not stagger twenty rows for 1.4 seconds.


## 06.31 — TEXT REVEALS

No per-letter theatrical animation for normal content.

Allowed:

- block;
- line;
- phrase.

Text should become readable quickly.

Hero headings may use masked line reveals where the static text exists from first paint.

No JS-generated letter soup.


## 06.32 — INITIAL PAGE LOAD

Critical rule:


### NEVER HIDE PRIMARY CONTENT UNTIL JAVASCRIPT RUNS.

The server-delivered page already contains:

- H1;
- lead;
- CTA;
- navigation.

Animation is additive.

If JS fails:

nothing appears “stuck at opacity: 0.”


## 06.33 — HERO CHOREOGRAPHY

The hero has one deliberate sequence.

```text
T = 0
```

Readable UI exists.

Logo/navigation/headline visible.

```text
T = 0–300ms
```

Origin node gains emphasis.

COLOMIERS


```text
T = 160–650ms
```

Latent routes appear.

Not violently drawn.

They resolve from low contrast.

```text
T = 280–700ms
```

Discipline labels establish branches.

```text
T = 400–900ms
```

Selected/primary CTA state becomes visually settled.


## 06.34 — HERO CHOREOGRAPHY RULE

User may:

- click;
- scroll;
- navigate

at **T = 0**.

Never block user control until intro completes.


## 06.35 — RETURN VISITS

Do not replay a grand intro on every page load.

The hero sequence should be subtle enough not to annoy repeated visitors.

Potentially:

first route entry has fuller 900ms treatment.

History/back navigation uses shorter state restoration.

No cookie required merely to suppress animation.


## 06.36 — HERO POINTER RESPONSE

Fine-pointer desktop only.

Allowed:

tiny route-field reaction.

Maximum displacement:

approximately:

4–12px

or similarly restrained deformation.

No huge object chasing cursor.

Touch:

no fake hover substitute.


## 06.37 — INTENT SELECTOR MOTION

User selects:

**ESSAYER LE MMA**

Sequence:


### 0ms

selection gets immediate visual state.


### 0–140ms

node activates.


### 80–260ms

relevant route becomes active.


### 100–300ms

non-relevant paths reduce emphasis.


### 160–360ms

next question appears.

Total:

roughly **360ms**.

User doesn't wait for it.


## 06.38 — INTENT SWITCH

If user immediately changes:

MMA → BOXE,

the current animation is canceled.

New state begins from current visual state.

Never queue:

MMA animation then Boxe animation

after the user has already chosen Boxe.


## 06.39 — INTERRUPTION RULE


### LATEST INTENT WINS.

Any state animation triggered by user input must be interruptible.

This applies to:

- selector;
- filters;
- recommendation;
- menu;
- comparison;
- planning.

No animation queues.


## 06.40 — REVERSIBILITY

When possible, state transitions should be reversible.

Example:

select Portet → change criteria → Minimes

The visual system should transform from Portet state to Minimes state.

Not:

reset everything to blank → replay entire sequence.

Continuity improves comprehension.


## 06.41 — ROUTE DRAWING

Default SVG route animation:


### Latent path

Already present at low contrast.


### Selection

Selected path visually resolves from origin toward destination.

Potential techniques:

- stroke-dash offset for short local draw;
- clipping/masking;
- segment activation.

No route should visibly draw for 2–3 seconds.

Target:

180–420ms

depending path significance.


## 06.42 — ROUTE THICKNESS

Do not animate actual SVG geometry unnecessarily.

Prefer state transition through:

- stroke color;
- opacity;
- possibly duplicated overlay path whose opacity changes.

If stroke-width animation profiles poorly:

replace with layered paths.


## 06.43 — RED RESOLUTION

This is one of the most meaningful transitions on the site.

Before selection:

Steel.

After defensible recommendation:

Signal Red progresses along the resolved path.

Sequence:

```text
STEEL
│
├─ user inputs criteria
│
├─ computation/result established
│
└─ SIGNAL RED route resolves
```

The red must appear **after** the answer exists.

Not before.

This behavior reinforces:


### RED IS EARNED.


## 06.44 — PROXIMITY ENGINE CHOREOGRAPHY

The engine is the motion climax.

But clarity remains first.

Potential sequence:


### INPUT

controls respond immediately.


### EVALUATE

multiple routes reduce in prominence.

Duration:

~200ms.


### CONVERGE

remaining valid route(s) reorganize.

Duration:

~300–420ms.


### ARRIVE

destination node resolves.

Duration:

~180–240ms.


### EXPLAIN

reason text appears.

Duration:

~160–260ms.

Total perceived transformation:

roughly **500–700ms**, overlapping rather than sequentially stacked.


## 06.45 — NEVER FAKE COMPUTATION

Do not show:

ANALYSING...

for 1.2 seconds

if the result exists synchronously.

If computation takes 2ms:

respond immediately.

Artificial loading undermines our truth philosophy.


## 06.46 — REAL ASYNC COMPUTATION

If live schedule/routing data genuinely requires a network request:

show minimal progress.

Example:

ON VÉRIFIE LES CRÉNEAUX…

Only while actual work happens.

If cached answer is available:

render it.


## 06.47 — NO MATCH

Motion must not make failure look broken.

Invalid routes quietly recede.

A dashed unresolved endpoint remains.

Copy:


### PAS DE CORRESPONDANCE EXACTE.

Then alternatives become visible.

Duration:

~200–300ms.

No shaking.

No red flashing.


## 06.48 — CLUB COMPARISON TRANSITIONS

When switching focus:

Minimes ↔ Portet,

we want continuity.

Not:

full card disappears → new card fades.

Instead:

- comparison axis persists;
- relevant rows update;
- destination photography changes;
- route terminus moves;
- active typographic label changes.

Approx:

`300–420ms`.


## 06.49 — PHOTOGRAPHIC TRANSITION

Image changes:

prefer restrained crossfade + tiny scale alignment.

Example:

opacity 0 → 1 scale .99 → 1

No 30% zoom.

No rotating picture cards.


## 06.50 — BLACK/WHITE → COLOR

If we use desaturation for latent vs selected photography:

transition is slow enough to perceive but not theatrical:

300–500ms

Color state must never carry essential selection information alone.


## 06.51 — FIRST-SESSION MOTION

The emotional section should **calm down**.

No complex state engine.

Sequence follows scrolling.

Each step:

- number;
- phrase;
- real image fragment.

Reveal:

`250–400ms`.

Maximum Y movement:

`12–24px`.

No timeline racing down the screen.


## 06.52 — PLANNING FILTER MOTION

This is a good use case for same-document View Transitions.

When user filters:

MMA + MERCREDI + SOIR

results can smoothly reflow while preserving spatial context.

Where supported:

document.startViewTransition(() => { updateScheduleResults(); });

Fallback:

immediate DOM update.

The current View Transition API specifically supports transitions between different DOM states and exposes mechanisms for skipping or controlling active transitions.


## 06.53 — PLANNING FILTER DURATION

Fast.

`180–300ms`.

Schedule data is utility.

Do not make users watch artistic choreography every time they select Tuesday.


## 06.54 — RESULT REORDERING

Movement should preserve the identity of a session where technically feasible.

A session moving from:

row 6 → row 2

should appear to move rather than disappear/reappear.

But only if implementation remains stable and accessible.

Otherwise:

simple fade update.

Clarity over cleverness.


## 06.55 — NAVIGATION HOVER

Desktop fine-pointer:

`120–180ms`.

Possible:

- underline/route extends;
- arrow shifts ~4px;
- label weight/contrast changes.

No background image preload takeover for every menu link.


## 06.56 — NAVIGATION FOCUS

Focus gets immediate visible response.

No positional motion required.

Focus indicator:

`0–100ms`.

A keyboard user's focus must never “chase” moving controls.


## 06.57 — HEADER COMPRESSION

Avoid animating `height` continuously on scroll.

Instead:

header can have fixed compact geometry and use:

- background opacity;
- border;
- internal transform;
- logo-size step at threshold if safe.

Avoid forcing layout recalculation every scroll frame.


## 06.58 — HEADER SCROLL STATE

Threshold-based.

Example:

TOP transparent/integrated AFTER HERO THRESHOLD solid material subtle border

Not continuously shrinking pixel-by-pixel with scroll.


## 06.59 — MOBILE MENU

Open:

`240–320ms`.

Suggested choreography:

1. overlay appears; 2. route spine resolves; 3. links enter with tiny stagger.

Close:

slightly faster.

`180–240ms`.


## 06.60 — MOBILE MENU ACCESSIBILITY

When opened:

- focus moves into menu;
- underlying content is not interactive;
- Escape closes;
- close button works;
- closing restores focus to trigger.

Animation does not control focus timing unnecessarily.

Focus can move as soon as logical menu state exists.


## 06.61 — PAGE TRANSITION PHILOSOPHY

The transition should answer:

**Where did this page come from?**

Example:

Homepage user selects:

**MMA**

then opens `/mma/`.

Instead of a generic fade:

the selected MMA route/label can be the shared continuity element.


## 06.62 — PAGE TRANSITION BASE

For generic internal navigation:

very restrained.

Old view:

opacity 1 → .94

New:

opacity .94 → 1 translateY 8px → 0

Total:

`220–320ms`.

No whole-page 100vh slides.


## 06.63 — ROUTE-SPECIFIC PAGE TRANSITION

For:

Homepage → MMA

selected route label/node may persist visually.

For:

Homepage → Quel Club

route system may become the destination engine.

For:

Planning → Club

selected club label may transition.

These shared transitions should be rare enough to remain meaningful.


## 06.64 — BACK NAVIGATION

Back must feel native.

If direction-aware native View Transition support is robust in target browsers, transitions may subtly reverse.

But:

history semantics are never changed to make animation easier.

The browser's Back button wins.


## 06.65 — VIEW TRANSITION TYPES

Modern View Transition interfaces now expose transition-type mechanisms, with MDN marking `ViewTransition.types` as newly broadly available in 2026.

Potential use:

forward back discipline club utility

This could allow different CSS treatment without creating a client router.

However:

this is enhancement.

The navigation must remain good when unsupported.


## 06.66 — EXTERNAL NAVIGATION

Minimes/Portet external links:

do not create fake continuity to a site whose visual system we do not control.

Normal navigation.

Maybe local button microstate before click.

No “portal animation.”


## 06.67 — ANCHOR SCROLLING

When linking to internal section:

- update URL appropriately;
- scroll without overshooting sticky header;
- use scroll-margin-top;
- reduced-motion users get instant movement.

No JS calculating bizarre offsets on every frame.


## 06.68 — SECTION REVEALS

We do not need every section to reveal.

Base content can simply exist.

Reserve reveal for:

- section thesis;
- meaningful sequence;
- visual relationship.


## 06.69 — DEFAULT REVEAL

Where useful:

opacity .85 → 1 translateY 16px → 0 duration 300–420ms

This should be almost felt rather than noticed.


## 06.70 — NO INVISIBLE WAITING STATES

Never place all below-fold content at:

```text
opacity: 0;
```

until IntersectionObserver runs.

If script fails, content must remain visible.

Preferred pattern:

static visible base.

Enhancement class added only when motion is initialized safely.


## 06.71 — REVEALS RUN ONCE

Ordinary section reveals:

once.

Scrolling down/up repeatedly should not make headings bounce into existence every time.

State changes may animate repeatedly.

Entrance decoration usually should not.


## 06.72 — IMAGE PARALLAX

Default:

none.

Potential approved case:

hero/destination environmental image with tiny internal movement.

Maximum effective offset:

~3–5% of image dimension.

Disabled:

- mobile if visually unstable;
- reduced motion;
- low-performance mode.


## 06.73 — STICKY SECTIONS

Potential maximum:

**one major sticky sequence per page.**

Likely candidate:

Proximity Engine.

Sticky content must not:

- trap focus;
- capture wheel;
- require huge dead scroll;
- make content inaccessible at 200% zoom;
- become unusable on short laptop screens.


## 06.74 — STICKY MOBILE

Assume:

**no long pinned cinematic sequence.**

Mobile height variation and browser chrome make these fragile.

Use direct sequential interaction.


## 06.75 — INTERACTION FEEDBACK MODEL

Every user action gets one or more:

VISUAL STATE TEXTUAL STATE POSITIONAL CONTINUITY

Example selecting MMA:

- visual: red node;
- textual: “MMA sélectionné”;
- positional: route converges.

Not every action needs all three.

But a meaningful action needs more than a tiny color flicker.


## 06.76 — PRESS STATE

Pointer/touch press:

`80ms`.

Possible:

scale(.99)

or 1px internal translation.

Subtle.


## 06.77 — HOVER MUST NEVER MOVE TARGET AWAY

Buttons do not flee/magnetically move toward pointer.

Precision means:

the hit target stays where the user aimed.


## 06.78 — DOUBLE-ACTION PROTECTION

While submitting contact form:

button may enter pending state.

Do not disable in a way that removes focus immediately unless necessary.

Show:

ENVOI…

prevent duplicate submission.

When success/failure arrives:

explicit state.


## 06.79 — FORM FIELD MOTION

Minimal.

Focus:

border/ring transition.

Error:

message appears.

No shaking inputs.

No bouncing success ticks.


## 06.80 — SUCCESS TRANSITION

Contact success can visually use our route:

DEMANDE

- ────────
- ÉQUIPE

Short route resolve:

`250–400ms`.

Then static.


## 06.81 — ERROR TRANSITION

No red flash.

No wiggle.

Error text appears immediately.

Potential node becomes unresolved/dashed.

Motion does not punish the user.


## 06.82 — 404 MOTION

Route travels toward missing destination.

Stops.

Alternative valid branches become available.

This can happen over:

~500ms.

But the 404 heading/actions are readable immediately.


## 06.83 — LOADING

No universal spinner.

For genuine data request:

small route evaluation state.

Maximum movement:

local.

Avoid full-screen loading state.


## 06.84 — CONTINUOUS ANIMATION

Default:


### NONE.

Nothing should float forever.

No perpetual:

- particles;
- rotating globe;
- pulsing buttons;
- breathing logos;
- route motion;
- background video autoplay masquerading as motion UI.

Once interface arrives:

it becomes calm.


## 06.85 — VIDEO

If real training video later appears:

- muted/autoplay only if justified and technically appropriate;
- pause controls as needed;
- no critical content solely inside video;
- image/poster fallback;
- reduced-data considerations;
- never auto-play loud audio.

Sound system, if ever desired, is a separate explicit decision.


## 06.86 — NO SOUND AT LAUNCH

Some S-tier references use sound successfully.

We do not inherit it by prestige.

For this project:


### NO INTERFACE SOUND BY DEFAULT.

Reasons:

- local-search utility;
- mobile context;
- gyms/offices/public space;
- accessibility;
- unnecessary payload/interaction complexity.

Potential future video sound remains user-controlled.


## 06.87 — REDUCED MOTION IS A DESIGNED MODE

This is non-negotiable.

W3C's current technique explicitly recommends `prefers-reduced-motion` to prevent non-essential motion, including JS-triggered motion, for users who request it.

Static styles come first.

Motion is enabled only when:

```text
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference) {
/* motion enhancement */
}
```

This is safer than building motion first and desperately trying to override it later.


## 06.88 — REDUCED-MOTION BEHAVIOR MATRIX

**Mechanism Default Reduced motion**

Hero routes resolve/draw final state immediately

Text entrances subtle movement

no translation; optional immediate/faint opacity

Page transitions continuity immediate navigation/swap

Selector routes move states switch immediately

Proximity Engine convergence immediate result

Planning reorder View Transition immediate reorder

Parallax subtle none

Image state tiny scale/fade static/faint fade

Menu slide/fade immediate/faint opacity

404 route short path static endpoint

Hover displacement

tiny none if motion-sensitive


## 06.89 — OPACITY IN REDUCED MOTION

Reduced motion does not mean every visual state change must be instantaneous if a gentle non-spatial opacity transition is harmless.

But default toward:

**less.**

Never replace movement with a 1-second fade.


## 06.90 — VIEW TRANSITIONS + REDUCED MOTION

If Astro's `<ClientRouter />` were ever used, Astro currently includes built-in handling that

disables its view-transition animation when `prefers-reduced-motion` is detected.

Because our preferred architecture is native MPA transitions, we must implement equivalent CSS behavior ourselves.

No assumption that framework tooling will save us.


## 06.91 — USER MOTION TOGGLE?

Not required at launch.

System-level preference is respected comprehensively.

A separate “Reduce motion” site control would be considered only if:

- user research supports it;
- interactive complexity grows significantly;
- it can be persisted cleanly.

Do not add settings UI merely to look accessibility-conscious.


## 06.92 — PERFORMANCE TIERS

The experience has conceptual capability tiers.


### TIER 0 — STATIC

HTML/CSS.

Full information.

No animation required.


### TIER 1 — CORE MOTION

CSS + SVG.

Basic route-state transitions.

This should represent nearly the complete branded experience.


### TIER 2 — ENHANCED

WAAPI / native View Transitions.

Spatial continuity.

Recommendation transformation.


### TIER 3 — EXPERIMENTAL

Canvas/WebGL only if later justified.

The site is never dependent on Tier 3.


## 06.93 — CAPABILITY DETECTION, NOT DEVICE PREJUDICE

Do not write:

if iPhone → effect if Android → no effect

Prefer:

- feature detection;
- media queries;
- pointer capability;
- reduced motion;
- Data Saver where reliably available;

- measured runtime behavior.

No user-agent art direction.


## 06.94 — FINE POINTER

Pointer-reactive enhancements only when:

```text
@media (hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)
```

Touch devices do not receive simulated cursor effects.


## 06.95 — OFFSCREEN PAUSE

Any continuous render work:

- stops when element leaves viewport;
- stops when tab becomes hidden;
- resumes only when needed.

Use:

- IntersectionObserver;
- visibilitychange.


## 06.96 — SHARED RAF LOOP

If advanced animation needs `requestAnimationFrame`:


### ONE SHARED SCHEDULER.

Not:

Hero RAF Selector RAF Cursor RAF Planning RAF Route RAF Image RAF

each running independently.

Components subscribe to one render clock.

This mirrors the disciplined rendering philosophy we previously identified in high-end motion tooling.


## 06.97 — RAF SHOULD SLEEP

If nothing is changing:

no frame loop.

An event-driven interface is preferable.

Only run continuously when an actual continuous visual needs it.


## 06.98 — DEVICE PIXEL RATIO

If Canvas/WebGL ever ships:

clamp render resolution.

Potential:

```text
const dpr = Math.min(window.devicePixelRatio, 1.5);
```

Exact cap determined by visual testing.

No need to render an abstract route field at DPR 3 on a phone.


## 06.99 — will-change

Use temporarily and locally.

Do not add:

* { will-change: transform; }

web.dev explicitly warns that excessive layer promotion can consume memory and degrade performance.


## 06.100 — ANIMATION PERFORMANCE TARGET

Target:

visually stable motion at the device's normal refresh rate without persistent dropped frames.

For traditional 60Hz displays that means roughly a 16.7ms frame budget, though modern devices may operate at higher refresh rates. web.dev uses 60 FPS as a common smoothness reference and emphasizes profiling dropped frames rather than assuming animation is performant.

We test.

We don't “feel” performance on one MacBook and declare victory.


## 06.101 — MAIN-THREAD BUDGET

Continuous visual logic should be tiny.

Avoid:

- repeated DOM measurement during scroll;
- forced synchronous layout;
- getBoundingClientRect() for 50 nodes every frame;
- reconstructing route SVG each frame;
- giant React rerenders on pointer movement.

Measure once.

Cache.

Recompute on meaningful events/resize.


## 06.102 — RESIZE

Debounce or schedule responsibly.

Route topology recalculates only when layout requires it.

Do not continuously reconstruct on every transient browser event.


## 06.103 — INTERSECTION OBSERVER

Use for:

- reveal entry;
- pausing advanced visuals;
- knowing relevant section;
- lazy activation.

Not a scroll-position replacement for everything.


## 06.104 — CSS SCROLL-DRIVEN ANIMATION

May be used as progressive enhancement if target support/testing is satisfactory at implementation time.

Never required for core comprehension.

Fallback:

static state.


## 06.105 — MOTION-ONLY JS BUDGET

Target:


### ≤ 15 KB gzip of custom motion-specific JavaScript

excluding actual business/selector logic.

Prefer considerably less.

A motion library that adds 40–70 KB for effects reproducible in native APIs fails by default.


## 06.106 — HERO ADVANCED GRAPHICS BUDGET

If SVG:

negligible.

If Canvas:

carefully profiled.

If WebGL:

must not delay:

- H1;
- navigation;
- CTA;
- primary imagery/content.

Advanced visual loads after useful interface.


## 06.107 — HYDRATION RULE

No hydration solely for:

- fade;
- hover;
- route color;
- sticky header;
- button arrow movement.

CSS handles these.

Hydration exists when there is **state/business logic**.


## 06.108 — PROXIMITY ENGINE ISLAND

This is the clearest legitimate island.

It owns:

- selection state;
- recommendation computation;
- URL/session synchronization;
- result explanation;
- accessible announcements.

Its animation is secondary to its logic.


## 06.109 — PLANNING ISLAND

Only if interactive filtering requires client behavior.

Base HTML should still contain useful planning/context.

Enhanced island adds:

- filter;
- quick matching;
- same-document transitions.


## 06.110 — INTENT SELECTOR

Could be:

- vanilla TS custom component;
- small framework island if shared state with Engine materially benefits.

Default:

prefer vanilla/custom element or minimal framework.

Do not choose React because it is familiar.

Choose the smallest stable architecture.


## 06.111 — SUGGESTED MOTION FILE STRUCTURE

```text
src/
styles/
motion/
```

tokens.css reduced-motion.css transitions.css route.css scripts/ motion/ preference.ts view-transitions.ts observers.ts scheduler.ts components/ route/ RouteGraph.astro RouteNode.astro RoutePath.astro components/ interactive/ IntentSelector.* ProximityEngine.* PlanningFilter.*

Do not create `animations.js` containing 2,000 unrelated lines.


## 06.112 — MOTION TOKEN API

Components consume semantic variables.

Example:

.intent-option { transition: color var(--motion-fast) var(--ease-standard), border-color var(--motion-fast) var(--ease-standard); }

Not:

transition: all .35s ease;


## 06.113 — transition: all

Default:


### FORBIDDEN.

Explicitly list animated properties.

Prevents accidental layout/paint animation later.


## 06.114 — ANIMATION CLEANUP

WAAPI animations:

- cancel when obsolete;
- dispose listeners;
- do not leak references;
- respect page lifecycle.

Canvas/render subscriptions:

unsubscribe when component destroyed.


## 06.115 — VIEW TRANSITION LIFECYCLE

If `<ClientRouter />` ever becomes justified, Astro provides lifecycle events including preparation, swap and page-load events, and it warns that scripts may need explicit reinitialization because bundled module scripts do not rerun like traditional document loads.

Therefore:

any decision to introduce ClientRouter requires an explicit **lifecycle audit**.

No “add `<ClientRouter />` and see what happens.”


## 06.116 — FOCUS AFTER PAGE CHANGE

Native MPA navigation already provides familiar browser/document behavior.

If client routing is ever introduced:

focus and route announcement must be tested explicitly.

Astro's ClientRouter currently includes a route announcer using the page `<title>`, then H1, then pathname as fallbacks.

This strengthens another already-locked requirement:


### EVERY PAGE MUST HAVE A CORRECT TITLE AND H1.

Accessibility and SEO converge again.


## 06.117 — MOTION + FOCUS RULE

Focus is never delayed until an animation completes.
